plan.md
# Plan: terse-reply reminder at each reply step

## Objective
Concise rule (top of `skills/poe/SKILL.md`) slips in later turns. Repeat a short, concrete version at each step where a mode writes its chat reply.

## Scope
In — `skills/poe/SKILL.md` only, one line appended to:
- Plan step 5
- Split step 6
- Execute step 7 (per-unit update)
- Execute step 9 (completion report; `resume` reuses it)
- `report` mode bullet (reply = path only)

Line wording (≈):
- Plan/split/execute 7: `Reply terse: ≤5 bullet fragments, no prose, don't repeat file contents.`
- Execute 9: same, ≤10 bullets.
- Report: `Reply: report path + any failure, nothing else.`
- `Next:` block not counted in the limit.

Out:
- `phase-worker.md`, README, files poe writes (plan.md etc.), `poe_report.py`, tests.
- Changing the existing `## Output style` section.

## Assumptions
- Builds on uncommitted `less_verboosity` edits to SKILL.md. Keep them.
- Bullet caps (5/10) are a starting guess; tune after the check below.

## Acceptance criteria
- Each listed step ends with the reminder line; nothing else in SKILL.md changed.
- `claude plugin validate .` passes.
- Manual check: plan-mode final reply on a toy task ≤5 bullets + `Next:` block, shorter than baseline.

## Risks
- Hard caps could squeeze out required content (blockers, failed tests). Mitigation: Output style's "never drop required content" stays; execute 9 gets 10.
- Effect unproven; may not help. Mitigation: before/after comparison.

## Validation
- `claude plugin validate .`
- Baseline: word count of final plan-mode reply in an existing transcript under `poe/*/logs/`.
- After: `claude --plugin-dir .`, `/poe:poe plan <toy task>`; compare reply length and bullet count.

# Phase 01: reply reminders

Depends on: none. Units run sequentially (U2 needs U1's edit).

## Unit 01-edit: add reminder lines to SKILL.md
- Goal: append one reminder line to each reply step listed in `plan.md` Scope.
- Read: `poe/poe-reply-reminders/plan.md`, `skills/poe/SKILL.md`, `git diff skills/poe/SKILL.md` (uncommitted `less_verboosity` edits; keep them).
- May change: `skills/poe/SKILL.md` only.
- Lines to add (end of each step/bullet):
  - Plan step 5, Split step 6, Execute step 7: `Reply terse: ≤5 bullet fragments, no prose, don't repeat file contents.`
  - Execute step 9: same, `≤10` bullets.
  - `report` mode bullet: `Reply: report path + any failure, nothing else.`
  - Plan 5 / Split 6: line must not land after the `Next:` fenced block (spec: nothing after block in reply; reminder may say `Next:` block not counted). Place it before the block's lead-in or within step text.
- Non-goals: `## Output style` section, `phase-worker.md`, README, `poe_report.py`, tests, any other wording.
- Verify:
  - `git diff skills/poe/SKILL.md` — only the 5 added lines beyond pre-existing edits (compare to `git stash`-free baseline: save `git diff` before editing to `progress/01-edit.baseline.diff`).
  - `claude plugin validate .` passes.
- Done when: 5 lines present, nothing else changed, validate passes.
- Notes: `poe/poe-reply-reminders/progress/01-edit.md`

## Unit 01-check: before/after reply length
- Goal: measure whether plan-mode final reply got shorter.
- Read: `plan.md` Validation; transcripts under `poe/*/logs/**/*.jsonl` (main transcripts only, not subagents).
- May change: nothing in repo outside `poe/poe-reply-reminders/progress/`. Run the toy task in the session scratchpad dir (or a `mktemp -d`), not the repo.
- Steps:
  - Baseline: final assistant text of a plan-mode turn from an existing transcript → word count, bullet count.
  - After: `cd <tmpdir> && claude -p --plugin-dir <repo> "/poe:poe plan <toy task>"` (toy task e.g. "add a --version flag to a hello.py CLI"; create minimal hello.py first). Capture final reply → word + bullet count.
  - If `claude -p` can't run (auth, refused), record as blocked; don't fake numbers.
- Non-goals: editing SKILL.md, tuning caps.
- Done when: notes hold baseline + after numbers (or explicit blocker), and verdict: ≤5 bullets + `Next:` block? shorter than baseline?
- Notes: `poe/poe-reply-reminders/progress/01-check.md`

#2 · 2026-11-19T13:52:20.871Z · user
Base directory for this skill: /Users/sbandyopadhyay/projects/SCRIPTS/custom_claude_plugins/poe/skills/poe # Phase-Orchestrated Work User request: You are the **orchestrator**. Keep the parent conversation focused on decisions, dependency management, integration, and verification. Delegate bounded implementation or research to fresh-context subagents rather than accumulating their intermediate work in the parent context. ## Output style Be concise. Sacrifice grammar for the sake of concision. show the rest (12,291 more characters)
- Applies to every reply and every file poe writes for the user: `plan.md`, phase briefs, `status.md`, `summary.md`.
- Bullets and fragments over prose.
- Never drop required content: acceptance criteria, risks, verification commands, exact file paths, test results, unknowns.

## Operating modes

- `plan <task>` (or a task description without a mode): write `plan.md` only, then stop so the user can review it. Do not split it into phases and do not edit implementation files.
- `split [plan-path]`: split a reviewed `plan.md` into phases and work units, then stop for user approval. Do not edit implementation files.
- `execute [plan-path]`: execute a plan that has been split and approved. If the plan has no phase briefs yet, say so and ask the user to run `split` first.
- When a mode is given no path, use the unambiguous active plan in `poe/`; otherwise ask for the path.
- `resume [plan-path]`: read `status.md` and the progress notes, compare them with the actual files, tests, and git diff, reconcile discrepancies, and continue only incomplete approved work. When it finishes or stops, write `summary.md`, copy the logs, and build the report as in Execute mode step 9.
- `report [plan-path]`: copy this session's logs as in the Logs section, build the report as in the Report section, and give the user the path of `report.html`. Do not edit the task's other files or any implementation files.
- For small, single-file or tightly coupled changes, recommend doing the work directly instead of forcing subagents. If the user still requests orchestration, comply.

## Files

Everything for one task lives in `poe/<short-task-slug>/`, relative to the current working directory (the directory the session was started in, even if that is not the repository root). Create `poe/` and the task directory if they do not exist (`mkdir -p`). Never overwrite the directory of a different task; pick a distinct slug.

```
poe/<short-task-slug>/
  plan.md              what will be built and why (written by `plan`)
  phases/NN-name.md    one brief per phase, listing its work units (written by `split`)
  status.md            state of every phase and unit, and the next action
  progress/NN-unit.md  running notes written by each worker during execution
  summary.md           execution summary, overwritten at the end of each `execute`/`resume` run
  report.html          step-by-step report built from the files above and logs/
  logs/<session-id>/   copies of the Claude Code transcripts of each session that worked on this task
    <session-id>.jsonl         main (orchestrator) transcript
    subagents/agent-*.jsonl    worker transcripts
    subagents/agent-*.meta.json
```

## Plan mode: write `plan.md`

1. Inspect only the relevant repository context, instructions, tests, and environment. Use read-only exploration subagents when exploration would otherwise overwhelm parent context.
2. Write `poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md` from what the user wants to do: objective, scope and exclusions, assumptions, the approach, acceptance criteria, risks, and how the result will be tested or validated. This file describes the work itself; it does not mention phases, subagents, or token budgets. Keep it short: do not restate the request, and give background only where it shapes a decision.
3. **Do not overengineer.** Plan the simplest approach that meets the stated requirements:
   - Build only what the user asked for. No speculative features, configuration options, plugin systems, or abstraction layers added "for later".
   - Prefer the tools, libraries, and patterns the repository already uses over introducing new ones.
   - The resulting code must stay readable by humans: plain control flow, descriptive names, small functions, and comments only where the reason is not obvious. Prefer a few clear lines over a clever one.
   - Keep the process as light as the code. Do not add steps, files, or reviews that the task does not need.
4. Do not silently choose a major architecture if it needs user approval; ask before writing it into the plan.
5. Show a short summary of the plan, state the path of `plan.md`, and stop. Point to the file; do not repeat its contents. The user reviews the file and may ask for changes; apply requested changes to `plan.md` and stop again. Do not proceed to the split until the user runs `split`. End the reply with a `Next:` line and a fenced block holding the exact command with the full plan path, so it can be pasted into a fresh `/clear`ed session. Nothing after the block:
   ````
   Next:
   ```
   /poe:poe split poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md
   ```
   ````

## Split mode: split `plan.md` into phases and work units

Read `plan.md` as it currently is on disk, including any edits the user made by hand. If phase briefs already exist from an earlier split and no unit has been started, replace them; if work has started, ask before changing them.

1. Split the plan into ordered **phases** with explicit dependencies, and each phase into **work units**. One work unit is handled by one fresh subagent and must be independently verifiable.
2. Size each unit so that one subagent can finish it in roughly 100k tokens of context, counting the files it must read, the edits, and the test output. Split a unit that looks larger; merge units that are trivially small. Use as few phases and units as the work actually needs.
3. Identify which units can run in parallel because they do not modify overlapping files or share mutable state. Run dependent or overlapping units sequentially, or isolate them in separate git worktrees and integrate deliberately.
4. Write one brief per phase at `phases/NN-name.md`. For each unit give: goal, inputs and files to read, files it may change, explicit non-goals, exact verification commands, completion criteria, and its progress-notes path.
5. Write `status.md` listing every phase and unit as pending.
6. Show the phase split, state where the files are, and ask for approval. Point to the files; do not repeat their contents. Do not start implementation in `split` mode. End the reply with the same `Next:` line and fenced block as Plan mode step 5, holding the execute command. Nothing after the block:
   ````
   Next:
   ```
   /poe:poe execute poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md
   ```
   ````

## Execute mode

1. Read `plan.md`, `status.md`, and the brief of the next phase. Verify that the plan is approved and the prerequisites of the next unit are satisfied.
2. Dispatch a **fresh non-fork subagent** for each work unit. Prefer the `poe:phase-worker` subagent bundled with this plugin (if it is not available under that name, look for `phase-worker`); otherwise use an appropriate built-in agent capable of doing the work. Do not use a fork for routine delegation: it inherits the parent's history.
3. Give the worker only: the goal, the phase-brief path, necessary repository paths, interfaces and dependencies, allowed files, completion criteria, and its progress-notes path. Do not paste the parent conversation or large tool output.
4. The worker implements or analyzes only its assigned unit and runs the relevant tests. It writes its progress notes **as it works**, not only at the end: it updates the notes file after each meaningful step, so that the notes are current if the worker stops, is compacted, or fails.
5. Run independent, non-overlapping units in parallel only when safe. Never silently overwrite another agent's edits.
6. Check the worker's claims against the actual files, test outputs, and git diff. For significant or high-risk changes, use a separate fresh-context reviewer before marking the unit verified. Fix or reassign failed units with a new scoped brief.
7. Update `status.md` after every unit. Keep the parent response concise: phase, tests, blockers, and next action. Reference files rather than returning large transcripts or repeating file contents.
8. Move to the next approved phase after verification. Stop for genuinely ambiguous requirements, security-sensitive changes, destructive operations, deployments, or decisions beyond the approved plan.
9. At completion, run integration tests, review the full change set, and report what passed, what remains uncertain, and where the files were saved; point to files rather than repeating their contents. Write the same report to `summary.md` (what passed, what remains uncertain, blockers, and file locations), overwriting any earlier one; `status.md` stays the source of truth for state. Then copy the logs (see Logs) and build the report (see Report).

## Logs

At the end of every mode (`plan`, `split`, `execute`, `resume`, `report`), and whenever execution stops for user input, copy this session's transcripts into the task directory:

```sh
dest="poe/<short-task-slug>/logs/f3f9f16e-8b33-46a2-a554-c53f8c11ca62"
src=$(find "${CLAUDE_CONFIG_DIR:-$HOME/.claude}/projects" -name "f3f9f16e-8b33-46a2-a554-c53f8c11ca62.jsonl" | head -1)
mkdir -p "$dest"
cp "$src" "$dest/"
[ -d "${src%.jsonl}/subagents" ] && cp -R "${src%.jsonl}/subagents" "$dest/"
```

- Re-running it overwrites the earlier copy with the current, longer transcript, so repeating it is safe.
- The copy is a snapshot: anything after it (such as the final reply of the turn) is missing until the next copy.
- If the transcript cannot be found (`src` is empty or `cp` fails), say so in the reply and carry on. A missing log never blocks the task.

## Report

At the end of `execute` and `resume` (after `summary.md` and the log copy), and in `report` mode, build `report.html` from the task directory:

```sh
python3 "/Users/sbandyopadhyay/projects/SCRIPTS/custom_claude_plugins/poe/skills/poe/scripts/poe_report.py" poe/<short-task-slug>
```

- The script writes `poe/<short-task-slug>/report.html` and prints its path. Re-running it replaces the earlier report.
- If it fails (for example, `python3` is missing or the command is refused), say so in the reply and carry on. A missing report never blocks the task.

## Progress notes

Each worker keeps one file, `progress/NN-unit.md`, and updates it throughout its run. `resume` and any follow-on worker rely on it, so it must make sense to an agent that has seen nothing else.

```
# Unit ID: status (in progress | done | partial | blocked)
## Done so far
## Files changed or produced
## Tests run and observed results
## Decisions and assumptions
## Next step / remaining work
```

When a unit is handed to a second worker, that worker reads the existing notes and continues in the same file.

## Context policy: about 100k tokens per agent

- 100k is a **soft target**, not a hard limit. Never claim to know exact token usage unless a tool reports it; otherwise judge by how much has been read and produced.
- Hand over at a natural boundary, not at a number. When a worker nears the target, it finishes the step it is in the middle of (an edit in flight, a test run, a debugging thread that is about to resolve), then brings its progress notes up to date and returns `partial`. Going moderately over the target to finish a coherent step is correct; stopping mid-edit to respect the number is not.
- The orchestrator then starts a **fresh non-fork worker** for the remainder, pointed at the progress notes. Do not keep resuming a bloated agent.
- Prefer this file-based handover to automatic compaction. Automatic compaction is a backstop: it can fire in the middle of a step and loses detail, which is why the notes are kept current throughout.
- Keep the parent lean: orchestration and short summaries only; delegate large searches, code reading, and log interpretation.
- Avoid unnecessary parallelism, repeated repository scans, duplicate reading, and verbose inter-agent messages.

## Constraints

- Follow the project's `CLAUDE.md`, security practices, and user instructions.
- Implement what the plan says and no more. Keep code simple and readable by humans; do not add abstractions or features the plan does not call for.
- Preserve existing user changes; check git status before edits. Do not commit, push, merge, delete, or deploy without authorization.
- Never infer that a test passed without observing its result. Preserve unknowns rather than fabricating evidence.
- If an agent has no delegation tool, say so and execute sequentially using the same file-based phase structure.

- Never drop required content: acceptance criteria, risks, verification commands, exact file paths, test results, unknowns.

## Operating modes

- `plan <task>` (or a task description without a mode): write `plan.md` only, then stop so the user can review it. Do not split it into phases and do not edit implementation files.
- `split [plan-path]`: split a reviewed `plan.md` into phases and work units, then stop for user approval. Do not edit implementation files.
- `execute [plan-path]`: execute a plan that has been split and approved. If the plan has no phase briefs yet, say so and ask the user to run `split` first.
- When a mode is given no path, use the unambiguous active plan in `poe/`; otherwise ask for the path.
- `resume [plan-path]`: read `status.md` and the progress notes, compare them with the actual files, tests, and git diff, reconcile discrepancies, and continue only incomplete approved work. When it finishes or stops, write `summary.md`, copy the logs, and build the report as in Execute mode step 9.
- `report [plan-path]`: copy this session's logs as in the Logs section, build the report as in the Report section, and give the user the path of `report.html`. Do not edit the task's other files or any implementation files.
- For small, single-file or tightly coupled changes, recommend doing the work directly instead of forcing subagents. If the user still requests orchestration, comply.

## Files

Everything for one task lives in `poe/<short-task-slug>/`, relative to the current working directory (the directory the session was started in, even if that is not the repository root). Create `poe/` and the task directory if they do not exist (`mkdir -p`). Never overwrite the directory of a different task; pick a distinct slug.

```
poe/<short-task-slug>/
  plan.md              what will be built and why (written by `plan`)
  phases/NN-name.md    one brief per phase, listing its work units (written by `split`)
  status.md            state of every phase and unit, and the next action
  progress/NN-unit.md  running notes written by each worker during execution
  summary.md           execution summary, overwritten at the end of each `execute`/`resume` run
  report.html          step-by-step report built from the files above and logs/
  logs/<session-id>/   copies of the Claude Code transcripts of each session that worked on this task
    <session-id>.jsonl         main (orchestrator) transcript
    subagents/agent-*.jsonl    worker transcripts
    subagents/agent-*.meta.json
```

## Plan mode: write `plan.md`

1. Inspect only the relevant repository context, instructions, tests, and environment. Use read-only exploration subagents when exploration would otherwise overwhelm parent context.
2. Write `poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md` from what the user wants to do: objective, scope and exclusions, assumptions, the approach, acceptance criteria, risks, and how the result will be tested or validated. This file describes the work itself; it does not mention phases, subagents, or token budgets. Keep it short: do not restate the request, and give background only where it shapes a decision.
3. **Do not overengineer.** Plan the simplest approach that meets the stated requirements:
   - Build only what the user asked for. No speculative features, configuration options, plugin systems, or abstraction layers added "for later".
   - Prefer the tools, libraries, and patterns the repository already uses over introducing new ones.
   - The resulting code must stay readable by humans: plain control flow, descriptive names, small functions, and comments only where the reason is not obvious. Prefer a few clear lines over a clever one.
   - Keep the process as light as the code. Do not add steps, files, or reviews that the task does not need.
4. Do not silently choose a major architecture if it needs user approval; ask before writing it into the plan.
5. Show a short summary of the plan, state the path of `plan.md`, and stop. Point to the file; do not repeat its contents. The user reviews the file and may ask for changes; apply requested changes to `plan.md` and stop again. Do not proceed to the split until the user runs `split`. End the reply with a `Next:` line and a fenced block holding the exact command with the full plan path, so it can be pasted into a fresh `/clear`ed session. Nothing after the block:
   ````
   Next:
   ```
   /poe:poe split poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md
   ```
   ````

## Split mode: split `plan.md` into phases and work units

Read `plan.md` as it currently is on disk, including any edits the user made by hand. If phase briefs already exist from an earlier split and no unit has been started, replace them; if work has started, ask before changing them.

1. Split the plan into ordered **phases** with explicit dependencies, and each phase into **work units**. One work unit is handled by one fresh subagent and must be independently verifiable.
2. Size each unit so that one subagent can finish it in roughly 100k tokens of context, counting the files it must read, the edits, and the test output. Split a unit that looks larger; merge units that are trivially small. Use as few phases and units as the work actually needs.
3. Identify which units can run in parallel because they do not modify overlapping files or share mutable state. Run dependent or overlapping units sequentially, or isolate them in separate git worktrees and integrate deliberately.
4. Write one brief per phase at `phases/NN-name.md`. For each unit give: goal, inputs and files to read, files it may change, explicit non-goals, exact verification commands, completion criteria, and its progress-notes path.
5. Write `status.md` listing every phase and unit as pending.
6. Show the phase split, state where the files are, and ask for approval. Point to the files; do not repeat their contents. Do not start implementation in `split` mode. End the reply with the same `Next:` line and fenced block as Plan mode step 5, holding the execute command. Nothing after the block:
   ````
   Next:
   ```
   /poe:poe execute poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md
   ```
   ````

## Execute mode

1. Read `plan.md`, `status.md`, and the brief of the next phase. Verify that the plan is approved and the prerequisites of the next unit are satisfied.
2. Dispatch a **fresh non-fork subagent** for each work unit. Prefer the `poe:phase-worker` subagent bundled with this plugin (if it is not available under that name, look for `phase-worker`); otherwise use an appropriate built-in agent capable of doing the work. Do not use a fork for routine delegation: it inherits the parent's history.
3. Give the worker only: the goal, the phase-brief path, necessary repository paths, interfaces and dependencies, allowed files, completion criteria, and its progress-notes path. Do not paste the parent conversation or large tool output.
4. The worker implements or analyzes only its assigned unit and runs the relevant tests. It writes its progress notes **as it works**, not only at the end: it updates the notes file after each meaningful step, so that the notes are current if the worker stops, is compacted, or fails.
5. Run independent, non-overlapping units in parallel only when safe. Never silently overwrite another agent's edits.
6. Check the worker's claims against the actual files, test outputs, and git diff. For significant or high-risk changes, use a separate fresh-context reviewer before marking the unit verified. Fix or reassign failed units with a new scoped brief.
7. Update `status.md` after every unit. Keep the parent response concise: phase, tests, blockers, and next action. Reference files rather than returning large transcripts or repeating file contents.
8. Move to the next approved phase after verification. Stop for genuinely ambiguous requirements, security-sensitive changes, destructive operations, deployments, or decisions beyond the approved plan.
9. At completion, run integration tests, review the full change set, and report what passed, what remains uncertain, and where the files were saved; point to files rather than repeating their contents. Write the same report to `summary.md` (what passed, what remains uncertain, blockers, and file locations), overwriting any earlier one; `status.md` stays the source of truth for state. Then copy the logs (see Logs) and build the report (see Report).

User request: $ARGUMENTS

```

## Plan mode: write `plan.md`

1. Inspect only the relevant repository context, instructions, tests, and environment. Use read-only exploration subagents when exploration would otherwise overwhelm parent context.
2. Write `poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md` from what the user wants to do: objective, scope and exclusions, assumptions, the approach, acceptance criteria, risks, and how the result will be tested or validated. This file describes the work itself; it does not mention phases, subagents, or token budgets. Keep it short: do not restate the request, and give background only where it shapes a decision.
3. **Do not overengineer.** Plan the simplest approach that meets the stated requirements:
   - Build only what the user asked for. No speculative features, configuration options, plugin systems, or abstraction layers added "for later".
   - Prefer the tools, libraries, and patterns the repository already uses over introducing new ones.
   - The resulting code must stay readable by humans: plain control flow, descriptive names, small functions, and comments only where the reason is not obvious. Prefer a few clear lines over a clever one.
   - Keep the process as light as the code. Do not add steps, files, or reviews that the task does not need.
4. Do not silently choose a major architecture if it needs user approval; ask before writing it into the plan.
5. Show a short summary of the plan, state the path of `plan.md`, and stop. Point to the file; do not repeat its contents. The user reviews the file and may ask for changes; apply requested changes to `plan.md` and stop again. Do not proceed to the split until the user runs `split`. End the reply with a `Next:` line and a fenced block holding the exact command with the full plan path, so it can be pasted into a fresh `/clear`ed session. Nothing after the block:
   ````
   Next:
   ```
   /poe:poe split poe/<short-task-slug>/plan.md
   ```
   ````

## Split mode: split `plan.md` into phases and work units

Read `plan.md` as it currently is on disk, including any edits the user made by hand. If phase briefs already exist from an earlier split and no unit has been started, replace them; if work has started, ask before changing them.

1. Split the plan into ordered **phases** with explicit dependencies, and each phase into **work units**. One work unit is handled by one fresh subagent and must be independently verifiable.
2. Size each unit so that one subagent can finish it in roughly 100k tokens of context, counting the files it must read, the edits, and the test output. Split a unit that looks larger; merge units that are trivially small. Use as few phases and units as the work actually needs.
3. Identify which units can run in parallel because they do not modify overlapping files or share mutable state. Run dependent or overlapping units sequentially, or isolate them in separate git worktrees and integrate deliberately.
4. Write one brief per phase at `phases/NN-name.md`. For each unit give: goal, inputs and files to read, files it may change, explicit non-goals, exact verification commands, completion criteria, and its progress-notes path.
5. Write `status.md` listing every phase and unit as pending.
6. Show the phase split, state where the files are, and ask for approval. Point to the files; do not repeat their contents. Do not start implementation in `split` mode. End the reply with the same `Next:` line and fenced block as Plan mode step 5, holding the execute command. Nothing after the block:
   ````
   Next:
   ```

# Plan: Concise poe output

## Objective
Cut verbosity of poe: chat replies (stdout) and written files (plan.md, phase briefs, status.md, summary.md, progress notes). Past plans ran 1200–1700 words; too long to review.

Instruction to add, verbatim: **"Be concise. Sacrifice grammar for the sake of concision."** Applies to all user-facing output.

## Scope
In:
- `skills/poe/SKILL.md`
  - New short section near top, e.g. `## Output style`: the verbatim instruction; applies to every reply and every file poe writes for the user (plan, briefs, status, summary). Bullets/fragments over prose.
  - Plan mode step 2: plan.md short; no restating request, no background beyond what shapes decisions.
  - Plan mode step 5 / split step 6 / execute step 7 & 9: replies point to file paths, don't repeat file contents.
  - **Next command.** Plan mode step 5 and split step 6: reply ends with a clearly labelled `Next:` line + fenced code block holding the exact command to run, with full plan path, so it can be pasted into a fresh `/clear`ed session with no other context. E.g.
    ````
    Next:
    ```
    /poe:poe split poe/<slug>/plan.md
    ```
    ````
    Plan → `split <path>`; split → `execute <path>`. Nothing else after the block.
- `agents/phase-worker.md`: same instruction for progress notes and final return message to orchestrator. Notes stay terse but must still let a fresh agent continue (keep the required headings).
- `README.md`: one line noting poe output is deliberately terse. Only if a natural place exists; skip otherwise.

Out:
- `poe_report.py`, tests, report.html format.
- Config option / toggle for verbosity.
- Changing code-comment or commit-message style.
- Next-command block for execute/resume/report (not requested).
- Rewriting existing plans in `poe/` or `plans/`.

## Assumptions
- "Interacting with the user" = chat replies + files user reads. Worker→orchestrator messages included (they feed parent context, so terse helps too).
- Concision must not drop required content: acceptance criteria, risks, verification commands, exact file paths, test results, unknowns.

## Approach
Text-only edits to two prompt files (+ maybe one README line). Place instruction once, prominently, in each file; tighten the few existing steps that invite long output. No duplicate wording sprinkled everywhere.

## Acceptance criteria
- Verbatim instruction present in `SKILL.md` and `phase-worker.md`.
- SKILL.md still lists required plan.md sections and progress-note headings.
- Plan and split replies end with `Next:` + fenced block holding full command with full plan path (checked in manual run).
- `claude plugin validate .` passes.
- `python3 -m unittest discover tests` still passes (sanity; no code change).
- Manual check: fresh `/poe:poe plan <small task>` run gives a noticeably shorter plan.md and reply than past plans (~1200+ words), with all required sections present.

## Risks
- Too terse → plans/notes ambiguous for fresh workers. Mitigation: explicit "don't drop required content" line.
- Model may ignore style instruction in long runs. Mitigation: put it near top of SKILL.md, not buried.

## Validation
Run the commands above; do manual plan-mode run on a throwaway task and compare length.
# poe: phase orchestrator for Claude Code

A Claude Code plugin with one skill (`poe`) and one subagent (`phase-worker`). It plans a task, splits it into work units sized for about 100k tokens each, and runs each unit in a fresh-context subagent that keeps file-based progress notes.

Requires Claude Code v2.1.296 or later.

## Installation

This repository is both the plugin and its own marketplace. Add the marketplace, then install the plugin:

```sh
claude plugin marketplace add sreejancodes/poe
claude plugin install poe@poe-tools
```

The same commands work inside a session as `/plugin marketplace add sreejancodes/poe` and `/plugin install poe@poe-tools`. Then run `/reload-plugins` or start a new session.

By default the plugin is installed at **user** scope, so it is available in every project. Add `--scope project` to enable it only for the current repository, recorded in `.claude/settings.json`.

### Update and uninstall

Third-party marketplaces do not update automatically. To get the latest version:

```sh
claude plugin marketplace update poe-tools
claude plugin update poe@poe-tools
```

To remove it:

```sh
claude plugin uninstall poe@poe-tools
claude plugin marketplace remove poe-tools
```

### Offering it to everyone on a project

Commit this to the project's `.claude/settings.json`:

```json
{
  "extraKnownMarketplaces": {
    "poe-tools": { "source": { "source": "github", "repo": "sreejancodes/poe" } }
  },
  "enabledPlugins": { "poe@poe-tools": true }
}
```

Once a collaborator trusts the folder, the marketplace is registered for them. They still need to run `claude plugin install poe@poe-tools --scope project` once.

## Usage

Plugin skills are namespaced, so the command is `/poe:poe`:

```text
/poe:poe plan Build an HPV ORF analysis pipeline with tests and reproducible outputs
/poe:poe split poe/hpv-orf-analysis/plan.md
/poe:poe execute poe/hpv-orf-analysis/plan.md
/poe:poe resume poe/hpv-orf-analysis/plan.md
/poe:poe report poe/hpv-orf-analysis/plan.md
```

- **Plan** writes `plan.md` from what you asked for, keeping the approach simple and the code human-readable, then stops. Review the file, edit it or ask for changes, and repeat until you are happy with it.
- **Split** takes the reviewed `plan.md` and splits it into phases and work units, each sized for one subagent at roughly 100k tokens. It then stops for approval.
- **Execute** hands each work unit to a fresh `poe:phase-worker` subagent, which writes progress notes as it works. The orchestrator verifies each unit before moving on.
- **Resume** uses the status file and progress notes to continue safely.
- **Report** copies the session's logs and (re)builds `report.html`, a step-by-step view of what the orchestrator and each worker did. Execute and resume also build it automatically at the end, after `summary.md` and the log copy; if the build fails, the reply says so and the task carries on.

Output is deliberately terse: replies point to files instead of repeating them, and plan and split replies end with the next command to paste.

## Files

Everything for one task is written under `poe/<task-slug>/` in the directory where Claude Code was started. `poe/` is created if it does not exist.

```
poe/<task-slug>/
  plan.md              what will be built and why
  phases/NN-name.md    one brief per phase, listing its work units
  status.md            state of every phase and unit
  progress/NN-unit.md  running notes written by each worker
  summary.md           execution summary, written at the end of execute/resume
  report.html          step-by-step report built from the files above and logs/
  logs/<session-id>/   copies of the Claude Code transcripts (.jsonl) of each session, including worker subagents
```

The copied logs contain the full conversation, including file contents and command output, and so does `report.html`. Normally add `poe/*/logs/` and `poe/*/report.html` (or all of `poe/`) to `.gitignore`.

To build the report by hand, without a Claude Code session (needs Python 3.9 or later, standard library only):

```sh
python3 <plugin>/skills/poe/scripts/poe_report.py poe/<task-slug>
```

`<plugin>` is this repository, or the installed plugin directory. The script writes `poe/<task-slug>/report.html` and prints its path. It is a single HTML file with no external resources, so it opens offline.

Tasks created by older versions in `plans/` can be moved with `mv plans/<slug> poe/<slug>`.

## Thinking text in the report

The report can only show thinking that Claude Code recorded in its transcripts. By default it records none, so each thinking step reads "thinking text not recorded". The overview and each transcript's totals show "thinking recorded: N of M blocks", so you can see whether it was on for a run.

To record it:

- Interactive sessions: set `"showThinkingSummaries": true` in `~/.claude/settings.json` (all projects) or in the project's `.claude/settings.json`. This also shows the summaries in the terminal. Workers are expected to inherit the setting from the session that starts them.
- Headless `claude -p` ignores that setting. Pass the flag instead: `claude -p --thinking-display summarized "..."`.

The recorded text is a summary of the reasoning, not the raw reasoning. Sessions that ran without it cannot be filled in later.

Steps labelled "narration" are short progress updates Claude Code writes alongside thinking; they are shown as they are and not counted as thinking.

## Context settings

The 100k figure is a soft target, not a hard cap. A worker nearing it finishes the step it is in the middle of, updates its progress notes, and returns. The orchestrator then starts a fresh worker from those notes.

`phase-worker` sets `autoCompactWindow: 150000`. This is a backstop above the target, so that automatic compaction does not interrupt a step at exactly 100k. It does not cap cumulative usage. Older Claude Code versions ignore the setting without warning.

The `CLAUDE_CODE_AUTO_COMPACT_WINDOW` environment variable takes precedence over a subagent's `autoCompactWindow`. Setting it to 100000 would make workers compact at 100k regardless, so leave it unset or set it above the target.

## Repository layout

```
.claude-plugin/plugin.json        plugin manifest
.claude-plugin/marketplace.json   marketplace listing this repo as the plugin source
skills/poe/SKILL.md               the /poe:poe orchestrator skill
skills/poe/scripts/poe_report.py  builds report.html for a task
agents/phase-worker.md            the worker subagent
```

To try local changes without installing: `claude --plugin-dir /path/to/this/repo`.

## References

- https://code.claude.com/docs/en/skills
- https://code.claude.com/docs/en/sub-agents

**Usage:** Per one mirror, the skill is stateful and assumes a codebase: it leaves a paper trail in CONTEXT.md and ADRs. It also saves nothing locally in the stateless grill-me variant. Installation is via npx skills add https://github.com/mattpocock/skills --skill grill-with-docs.

#23 · 2026-11-19T13:53:42.067Z · tool result · Bash
===== skills/productivity/grilling/SKILL.md --- name: grillingshow the rest (20,440 more characters)
description: Grill the user relentlessly about a plan, decision, or idea. Use when the user wants to stress-test their thinking, or uses any 'grill' trigger phrases.
---

Interview the user relentlessly until you reach a shared understanding. Map this as a **design tree**: every decision branches into the decisions that hang off it.

Work the tree in **rounds**. The **frontier** is every decision whose prerequisites are already settled: the questions you can ask _now_ without guessing at answers you haven't heard yet. Ask the whole frontier in one round: number each question and give your recommended answer. Then wait for the user's answers before the next round.

Format a round like so:

```
❓ **Q1** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

➡️ <your recommended answer>

---

❓ **Q2** - **<question title>**: <question body, might be multiple paragraphs, including multiple choices>

➡️ <your recommended answer>
```

Word each question so "yes" accepts your recommended answer.

Each round the user answers reshapes the tree: settled decisions push the frontier outward and unblock questions that depended on them. Recompute the frontier and ask the next round. A question whose answer depends on another question still open in this round belongs to a _later_ round, not this one.

Finding _facts_ is your job, never the user's. When a frontier question needs a fact from the environment (filesystem, tools, etc.), dispatch a sub-agent with a fresh context window to find it; don't ask the user for anything you could look up yourself. Don't block on it: a running exploration is an unsettled prerequisite, so only the questions downstream of it wait for the sub-agent to report; ask the rest of the frontier now. The _decisions_ are the user's: put each to them and wait.

The session is done when the frontier is empty: every branch of the design tree visited, nothing left silently assumed. Do not act on it until the user confirms you have reached a shared understanding.
===== skills/engineering/domain-modeling/SKILL.md
---
name: domain-modeling
description: Build and sharpen a project's domain model. Use when discussing codebase terminology, writing or editing a GLOSSARY.md, or recording or editing an ADR.
---

# Domain Modeling

Actively build and sharpen the project's domain model as you design. This is the *active* discipline: challenging terms, inventing edge-case scenarios, and writing the glossary and decisions down the moment they crystallise. (Merely *reading* `GLOSSARY.md` for vocabulary is not this skill: that's a one-line habit any skill can do. This skill is for when you're changing the model, not just consuming it.)

## File structure

Most repos have a single context:

```
/
├── GLOSSARY.md
├── docs/
│   └── adr/
│       ├── 0001-event-sourced-orders.md
│       └── 0002-postgres-for-write-model.md
└── src/
```

If a `GLOSSARY-MAP.md` exists at the root, the repo has multiple contexts. The map points to where each one lives:

```
/
├── GLOSSARY-MAP.md
├── docs/
│   └── adr/                          ← system-wide decisions
├── src/
│   ├── ordering/
│   │   ├── GLOSSARY.md
│   │   └── docs/adr/                 ← context-specific decisions
│   └── billing/
│       ├── GLOSSARY.md
│       └── docs/adr/
```

Create files lazily: only when you have something to write. If no `GLOSSARY.md` exists, create one when the first term is resolved. If no `docs/adr/` exists, create it when the first ADR is needed.

## During the session

### Challenge against the glossary

When the user uses a term that conflicts with the existing language in `GLOSSARY.md`, call it out immediately. "Your glossary defines 'cancellation' as X, but you seem to mean Y. Which is it?"

### Sharpen fuzzy language

When the user uses vague or overloaded terms, propose a precise canonical term. "You're saying 'account': do you mean the Customer or the User? Those are different things."

### Discuss concrete scenarios

When domain relationships are being discussed, stress-test them with specific scenarios. Invent scenarios that probe edge cases and force the user to be precise about the boundaries between concepts.

### Cross-reference with code

When the user states how something works, check whether the code agrees. If you find a contradiction, surface it: "Your code cancels entire Orders, but you just said partial cancellation is possible. Which is right?"

### Update GLOSSARY.md inline

When a term is resolved, update `GLOSSARY.md` right there. Don't batch these up: capture them as they happen. Use the format in [GLOSSARY-FORMAT.md](./GLOSSARY-FORMAT.md).

`GLOSSARY.md` should be totally devoid of implementation details. Do not treat `GLOSSARY.md` as a spec, a scratch pad, or a repository for implementation decisions. It is a glossary and nothing else.

### Offer ADRs sparingly

Only offer to create an ADR when all three are true:

1. **Hard to reverse**: the cost of changing your mind later is meaningful
2. **Surprising without context**: a future reader will wonder "why did they do it this way?"
3. **The result of a real trade-off**: there were genuine alternatives and you picked one for specific reasons

If any of the three is missing, skip the ADR. Use the format in [ADR-FORMAT.md](./ADR-FORMAT.md).
===== skills/engineering/domain-modeling/GLOSSARY-FORMAT.md
# GLOSSARY.md Format

## Structure

```md
# {Context Name}

{One or two sentence description of what this context is and why it exists.}

## Language

**Order**:
{A one or two sentence description of the term}
_Avoid_: Purchase, transaction

**Invoice**:
A request for payment sent to a customer after delivery.
_Avoid_: Bill, payment request

**Customer**:
A person or organization that places orders.
_Avoid_: Client, buyer, account
```

## Rules

- **Be opinionated.** When multiple words exist for the same concept, pick the best one and list the others under `_Avoid_`.
- **Keep definitions tight.** One or two sentences max. Define what it IS, not what it does.
- **Only include terms specific to this project's context.** General programming concepts (timeouts, error types, utility patterns) don't belong even if the project uses them extensively. Before adding a term, ask: is this a concept unique to this context, or a general programming concept? Only the former belongs.
- **Group terms under subheadings** when natural clusters emerge. If all terms belong to a single cohesive area, a flat list is fine.

## Single vs multi-context repos

**Single context (most repos):** One `GLOSSARY.md` at the repo root.

**Multiple contexts:** A `GLOSSARY-MAP.md` at the repo root lists the contexts, where they live, and how they relate to each other:

```md
# Glossary Map

## Contexts

- [Ordering](./src/ordering/GLOSSARY.md): receives and tracks customer orders
- [Billing](./src/billing/GLOSSARY.md): generates invoices and processes payments
- [Fulfillment](./src/fulfillment/GLOSSARY.md): manages warehouse picking and shipping

## Relationships

- **Ordering → Fulfillment**: Ordering emits `OrderPlaced` events; Fulfillment consumes them to start picking
- **Fulfillment → Billing**: Fulfillment emits `ShipmentDispatched` events; Billing consumes them to generate invoices
- **Ordering ↔ Billing**: Shared types for `CustomerId` and `Money`
```

The skill infers which structure applies:

- If `GLOSSARY-MAP.md` exists, read it to find contexts
- If only a root `GLOSSARY.md` exists, single context
- If neither exists, create a root `GLOSSARY.md` lazily when the first term is resolved

When multiple contexts exist, infer which one the current topic relates to. If unclear, ask.
===== skills/engineering/domain-modeling/ADR-FORMAT.md
# ADR Format

ADRs live in `docs/adr/` and use sequential numbering: `0001-slug.md`, `0002-slug.md`, etc.

Create the `docs/adr/` directory lazily: only when the first ADR is needed.

## Template

```md
# {Short title of the decision}

{1-3 sentences: what's the context, what did we decide, and why.}
```

That's it. An ADR can be a single paragraph. The value is in recording *that* a decision was made and *why*, not in filling out sections.

## Optional sections

Only include these when they add genuine value. Most ADRs won't need them.

- **Status** frontmatter (`proposed | accepted | deprecated | superseded by ADR-NNNN`): useful when decisions are revisited
- **Considered Options**: only when the rejected alternatives are worth remembering
- **Consequences**: only when non-obvious downstream effects need to be called out

## Numbering

Scan `docs/adr/` for the highest existing number and increment by one.

## When to offer an ADR

All three of these must be true:

1. **Hard to reverse**: the cost of changing your mind later is meaningful
2. **Surprising without context**: a future reader will look at the code and wonder "why on earth did they do it this way?"
3. **The result of a real trade-off**: there were genuine alternatives and you picked one for specific reasons

If a decision is easy to reverse, skip it: you'll just reverse it. If it's not surprising, nobody will wonder why. If there was no real alternative, there's nothing to record beyond "we did the obvious thing."

### What qualifies

- **Architectural shape.** "We're using a monorepo." "The write model is event-sourced, the read model is projected into Postgres."
- **Integration patterns between contexts.** "Ordering and Billing communicate via domain events, not synchronous HTTP."
- **Technology choices that carry lock-in.** Database, message bus, auth provider, deployment target. Not every library: just the ones that would take a quarter to swap out.
- **Boundary and scope decisions.** "Customer data is owned by the Customer context; other contexts reference it by ID only." The explicit no-s are as valuable as the yes-s.
- **Deliberate deviations from the obvious path.** "We're using manual SQL instead of an ORM because X." Anything where a reasonable reader would assume the opposite. These stop the next engineer from "fixing" something that was deliberate.
- **Constraints not visible in the code.** "We can't use AWS because of compliance requirements." "Response times must be under 200ms because of the partner API contract."
- **Rejected alternatives when the rejection is non-obvious.** If you considered GraphQL and picked REST for subtle reasons, record it; otherwise someone will suggest GraphQL again in six months.
===== docs/engineering/grill-with-docs.md
## What it does

`grill-with-docs` interviews you about a plan or design until you and the [agent](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/agent) share one understanding of it, and writes the vocabulary and the hard decisions into your repo while it does. It is the same interview [grill-me](https://aihero.dev/skills-grill-me) runs (a round of questions, then wait, then the next round), pointed at a codebase.

It is **[stateful](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/stateful)**. Every other grilling skill leaves the [session](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/session) in your head; this one leaves files on disk. When a term resolves, the skill writes it to `GLOSSARY.md` at once, not in a batch at the end. When a decision passes three gates, the skill writes it as an ADR. That is the whole difference, and it also causes most of the trouble people have with the skill. The artifacts are real files in a real repo, so they can be missing when you expected them, and they can drift when more than one person writes them.

## When to reach for it

You invoke this by typing `/grill-with-docs`, and the agent won't reach for it on its own.

Reach for it at the start of a change, in a repo, when the plan is still fuzzy and the words for the thing are not settled yet. It is the single-session tool. Which grilling skill you want depends on what is in front of you:

| What you have | Reach for |
| --- | --- |
| You aren't working in a working directory at all | [grill-me](https://aihero.dev/skills-grill-me) |
| A repo, and a change you can settle in one session | `grill-with-docs` |
| An effort too big to hold in one session (a greenfield build, a large feature) | [wayfinder](https://aihero.dev/skills-wayfinder) |
| A repo with no domain docs at all, and no particular feature in mind | `grill-with-docs`, aimed at the repo rather than a change |
| A decision blocked on knowledge in someone else's head | [to-questionnaire](https://aihero.dev/skills-to-questionnaire) |

The wayfinder split comes down to session count: `/grill-with-docs` for single-session planning, `/wayfinder` for multi-session planning.

## Prerequisites

The skill writes into your repo, so you need to be somewhere it is safe to write. Resolved terms go to a `GLOSSARY.md` glossary at the root, or to the relevant context's `GLOSSARY.md`, if a `GLOSSARY-MAP.md` at the root marks the repo as multi-context. Decisions go to `docs/adr/`. The skill creates both only when it needs them. Nothing exists until the first term or decision is settled, so you set up nothing in advance.

It also needs two other skills present, because its own `SKILL.md` is one line that delegates to them. [grilling](https://aihero.dev/skills-grilling) supplies the interview, and [domain-modeling](https://aihero.dev/skills-domain-modeling) supplies the writing. Installing `grill-with-docs` alone gets you a skill that does not work.

## The paper trail

Three things come out of a session, and they are not equal.

| What resolved | Where it lands |
| --- | --- |
| A term: the project's own word for a thing | `GLOSSARY.md`, inline, the moment it resolves |
| A decision that is hard to reverse, surprising without context, and a real trade-off | An ADR under `docs/adr/` |
| Everything else you decided | The conversation, and nowhere else |

That third row is the one that catches people out. `GLOSSARY.md` is only a glossary. It holds no implementation details, no [spec](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/spec), and no scratch notes. An ADR needs all three conditions at once, so most decisions do not qualify and most sessions produce none. A session that yields a sharper glossary and zero ADRs is working as designed, but it means most of what you agreed exists only in the [context window](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/context-window) you agreed it in. Hand that same conversation to [to-spec](https://aihero.dev/skills-to-spec) rather than [clearing](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/clearing) it.

The glossary is the main output. This skill builds domain language: the project's own words, agreed once, so you, the agent and your colleagues do not have to work them out again. Not everyone agrees that this improves agent performance. The strongest objection is that a term and its plain-English expansion get the same result from the [model](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/model), and that the vocabulary mainly shortens communication between the humans who share it. On that view the glossary is still valuable, but the value goes to the humans.

## Common questions

**Should I use this or `/wayfinder`?**
Scope decides it. Use this for anything you can settle in one session; use [wayfinder](https://aihero.dev/skills-wayfinder) when the effort is too big to hold in one, and it charts the work as a map of decision [tickets](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/ticket) first. Wayfinder is slower and denser, and reaching for it on a well-scoped feature is the common mistake. It does not replace this skill, and it can start a grilling session for the parts of the map that suit one.

**It ran, but no `GLOSSARY.md` and no ADRs appeared.**
There are two known causes. The first is that nothing qualified. ADRs need all three gates, and a session about a change with no new vocabulary has nothing to write. The second is a real bug. When the skill runs inside another orchestration layer (a spec-driven-development wrapper, a multi-agent framework, a rule that invokes it as a step in someone else's pipeline), users report that the file-writing half silently does not happen, while the interview still runs. The bug is filed and unfixed. If you are in that setup, check the working directory before you trust the session's output.

**It asked everything at once, with no recommendations, and never mentioned `GLOSSARY.md`.**
That is the skill failing to load its two dependencies. Because `SKILL.md` is a one-line delegation, an agent that does not pick up [grilling](https://aihero.dev/skills-grilling) and [domain-modeling](https://aihero.dev/skills-domain-modeling) guesses at what grilling means, and you get every question at once with no structure. Partial loading is more confusing. `grilling` loads, `domain-modeling` does not, and you get a good interview with no paper trail. How often it happens depends on the model and the [effort](https://www.aihero.dev/ai-coding-dictionary/effort) level, and it is the most reported problem with this skill. If you suspect it, ask the agent directly which skills it loaded.

**Where did all my other decisions go?**
Into the conversation only. This is the most serious open complaint about the skill. The glossary is not a spec, most answers do not earn an ADR, and no record links each resolved answer to a spec, a ticket and a test. Later steps soften precise answers (ordering guarantees, negative requirements, numeric defaults) into weaker prose, and the result can look complete while missing the thing you decided. For now, keep the session and feed it straight to [to-spec](https://aihero.dev/skills-to-spec). Then re-read the spec against your own answers rather than assuming it captured them.

**Can I point it at an existing repo that has no docs at all?**
Yes. This is the right skill for a codebase with no ADRs, no domain language and no design principles: invoke it and say "help me document my repo". Users often pair it with [improve-codebase-architecture](https://aihero.dev/skills-improve-codebase-architecture) for building or repairing a `GLOSSARY.md`. Expect to steer it. It reads code and asks you about what it finds, and you decide which of the words already in the codebase are the right ones.

**What should I do when the session ends?**
The skill's closing message is often open-ended, which is a known problem. In the main flow the answer is [to-spec](https://aihero.dev/skills-to-spec), in the same conversation. If the change is small enough to build immediately, go straight to [implement](https://aihero.dev/skills-implement) instead.

**Why is it called that?**
Nobody is happy with the name. There is an open suggestion to rename it `grill-domain-model`, which describes the behaviour more accurately. Nothing has moved on it. If a rename ever lands, the docs page moves with it and the URL changes.

## It's working if

- `GLOSSARY.md` changes *during* the session, term by term, rather than appearing in one lump at the end.
- The glossary reads as pure vocabulary (your project's words with tight definitions) and contains no implementation detail or spec-like prose.
- Questions the codebase can answer get answered by reading the codebase, not asked of you.
- You get few or no ADRs, and the ones you get are decisions you would be annoyed to have to argue again.
- It challenges a word you used because your existing glossary defines it differently.

## Where it fits

`grill-with-docs` is the head of the main build chain:

```txt
grill-with-docs → to-spec → to-tickets → implement → code-review → retro
```

It comes before anything is written down as a spec. It produces the shared understanding and settled vocabulary that [to-spec](https://aihero.dev/skills-to-spec) then synthesises without interviewing you again. Its close neighbours are [grill-me](https://aihero.dev/skills-grill-me), the same interview with no repo and no files, and [domain-modeling](https://aihero.dev/skills-domain-modeling), the glossary-and-ADR discipline it drives; both use the [grilling](https://aihero.dev/skills-grilling) primitive for the interview. Upstream of it, [wayfinder](https://aihero.dev/skills-wayfinder) charts efforts too large for one session and can hand parts of the map back down to it. When you're unsure which skill or flow fits, [ask-matt](https://aihero.dev/skills-ask-matt) routes you.

#24 · 2026-11-19T13:54:00.467Z · thinking
I'm settling the key design points now. One known issue stands out: when the skill runs inside another orchestration layer, the file-writing step can silently fail. The plan.md file also looks like the fix for losing earlier decisions, since it serves as the spec, so poe plan maps onto grilling, domain modeling, and writing to the spec. I'm weighing whether to vendor the grilling and domain-modeling text into poe, depend on the user having mattpocock's skills installed, or inline condensed instructions in SKILL.md with attribution. Inlining looks best, since it avoids the third-party dependency and the orchestration bug. I'm also questioning whether grilling should be on by default in plan mode, which would make planning multi-turn rather than write-then-stop, and where the GLOSSARY.md and ADR docs should live. I'm weighing where the GLOSSARY.md and ADR docs should live: at the repo root, as upstream does, so they persist across tasks, or inside the task directory. Repo root fits better since their value is cross-task, though cwd may not be the repo root. I'm also planning a "Decisions" section in plan.md to capture resolved questions, and having the split and phase-worker steps read GLOSSARY.md when it exists. ---
name: phase-worker
description: Implements or analyses one tightly scoped work unit from a poe phase plan, validates it, and keeps running progress notes for the parent orchestrator.
autoCompactWindow: 150000
---

You are a **single-unit execution worker**, not a project orchestrator.

**Be concise. Sacrifice grammar for the sake of concision.** This applies to your progress notes and your final return message to the orchestrator. Prefer bullets and fragments over prose. Never drop required content: keep the progress-notes headings and enough detail for a fresh agent to continue (exact paths, commands, test results, unknowns).

1. Read the phase brief and only the necessary repository files. If the progress-notes file for your unit already exists, read it first and continue from where it stops. Respect all existing repository instructions and preserve unrelated user changes.
2. Execute the assigned unit only. Do not expand scope or initiate unrelated subagents. Treat the phase brief's file boundaries and acceptance criteria as binding.
3. Keep the code simple and readable by humans: plain control flow, descriptive names, and no abstractions, options, or features the brief does not call for.
4. Write progress notes to the path supplied by the parent **as you work**: update the file after each meaningful step, not only at the end. Include status (in progress/done/partial/blocked), what is done so far, files changed, commands and observed test results, decisions, and the next step. Another agent with no other context must be able to continue from these notes.
5. Verify your result with the specified tests or checks; state explicitly when tests cannot be run.
6. Aim to finish within roughly 100k tokens of context. This is a soft target. If you are nearing it, or the unit proves broader than expected, finish the step you are in the middle of, bring the notes up to date, and return `partial` so that a fresh worker can take over. Do not stop mid-edit to respect the number, and do not carry on indefinitely either.
7. The `autoCompactWindow` of 150000 is a backstop above the target, not a budget and not a hard consumption limit. Never infer an exact token count from it.
8. Do not commit, push, merge, deploy, or perform destructive operations without explicit authorization.

#31 · 2026-11-19T13:56:22.768Z · thinking
I'm drafting the plan for a skill called poe-grill-with-docs, keeping it short. The core idea is that plan mode will interview the user in grill rounds and keep GLOSSARY.md and the ADRs current before writing plan.md, with every resolved decision recorded there. I'm starting the scope section with a new GRILLING.md reference file that plan mode loads, a condensed version of the upstream approach. I'm now filling in the GRILLING.md contents: the design tree and frontier format with rounds, a recommended answer for each question, and facts gathered by read-only subagents rather than asked of the user. I'm also covering how domain terms get challenged and sharpened, when an ADR is warranted, and whether a small GLOSSARY-MAP.md one-liner is worth keeping. I'm now working through the rest of the plan: the step that has the plan mode write plan.md once the grilling is done, with a Decisions section listing each resolved answer and links to any ADRs or glossary terms it touched. I'm also checking how this interacts with the existing don't-overengineer rule, since grilling's recommendations should already respect it, and how the plan gets revised when the user changes the request. Plan mode may create and edit GLOSSARY.md and docs/adr/, so I'm thinking about where those boundaries sit. I'm going through the rest of the plan: the operating modes bullet needs updating so plan mode can also create and edit GLOSSARY.md and docs/adr/ at the project root, and the Files section should note that those live outside poe/. I'm also deciding whether the logs copy rule needs changing, since each grilling round stops for input, and I'm leaning toward keeping the current rule as is. I'm weighing whether to store the grilling answers somewhere safer than chat context, since a cleared session would lose them. One option is a separate running log file, but that adds an artifact and feels heavier than needed. A simpler route is to append each answered round to a Decisions section in plan.md as it happens, then fill in the rest after the user confirms, which keeps everything in one file and survives a /clear. I'm now settling the split step: each unit's inputs will include GLOSSARY.md and any relevant ADRs when they exist, so the workers pick them up through their briefs without needing any change on the worker side. Last, I'm making sure the README's plan usage line and its Filesshow the rest (1,321 more characters)

I'm weighing the risks of this design: plan mode becomes multi-turn and longer, so I'll lean on recommended answers and a "yes" shortcut to keep it light. I also need to make sure the skill reads GRILLING.md explicitly and that glossary edits actually happen during the session, and I'll note in the README that the new files land outside poe/ and are only created when needed.

#39 · 2026-11-19T13:57:05.997Z · assistant
I couldn't save `poe/poe-grill-with-docs/plan.md`. Auto mode's safety check returned no verdict on the file write, so it was blocked, and that block can't be retried in this mode. The plan is drafted but not on disk. The session log copy did work and is in `poe/poe-grill-with-docs/logs/`. **What I learned about grill-with-docs:** - The skill itself is only one line that loads two other skills from the same repo: - **`grilling`**: asks questions in rounds. Each round covers every decision that can be made now, numbered, with a recommended answer, and replying "yes" accepts all of them. The agent looks up facts itself; the user only makes decisions. It ends when nothing is left open and the user confirms. - **`domain-modeling`**: writes each term to `GLOSSARY.md` as soon as it's agreed, and writes an ADR (a short note recording a decision and why) only for decisions that are hard to reverse, surprising, and a real trade-off. - Its own docs list a known bug: when another orchestrator runs it (poe is one), the doc writing is silently skipped. That's why I recommended copying the method in rather than depending on the upstream skills. - Its biggest open complaint is that most decisions end up only in the conversation. In poe, `plan.md` would fix that. **The plan, using your three answers (copy the method in, always on, docs at the cwd root):** - **New `skills/poe/GRILLING.md`:** a condensed version of the two skills plus their glossary and ADR formats, with credit to Matt Pocock and the MIT licence notice. - **`skills/poe/SKILL.md` plan mode** becomes:show the rest (1,571 more characters)
  1. Read existing `GLOSSARY.md` and `docs/adr/`, then read `GRILLING.md` as an explicit step.
  2. Ask one round, then stop and wait for answers.
  3. After each answered round, add the decisions to a `## Decisions` section in `plan.md` and update the glossary and ADRs. Nothing is held only in the conversation, so it survives a `/clear`.
  4. When nothing is left open, ask you to confirm, then write the full `plan.md` and end with the usual `Next:` block.
  - The old "ask before choosing a major architecture" step folds into grilling.
  - Split mode lists `GLOSSARY.md` and relevant ADRs as inputs for each work unit.
- **`README.md`:** describe the new plan behaviour, mention the new files, and credit the source.
- **Not included:** Matt's other skills (to-spec, wayfinder), an on/off flag, changes to `phase-worker.md` or the report script.
- **Checks:** `claude plugin validate .`, the existing unit tests, and a manual plan run in a scratch repo. The main check is that `GLOSSARY.md` changes during the session rather than appearing all at once at the end.

To get the file saved, either approve file writes (for example, switch out of auto mode) and tell me to retry, or I can paste the full `plan.md` here for you to save yourself.

#71 · 2026-11-19T14:10:48.031Z · assistant
I mean adding a short reminder right at the step where each mode writes its reply. Right now the rule is stated once, near the top of `SKILL.md`. The reply instructions sit much further down, in Plan step 5, Split step 6 and Execute steps 7 and 9, and none of them repeat it. For example, Plan step 5 could become: ``` 5. Show a short summary of the plan, state the path of `plan.md`, and stop. Reply terse: ≤5 bullet fragments, no prose, don't repeat file contents. ... ``` Split step 6 and Execute steps 7 and 9 would get the same line. The idea is that the rule then sits right next to the instruction the model reads just before replying, rather than many lines above it. A hard limit like "≤5 bullets" is also easier to follow than "be concise".show the rest (153 more characters)
This is untested and might not fix it. The way to check is to run plan mode once before the change and once after, and compare how long the replies are.
